> DEVELOPER

Work on Linear issue ABA-238:

<issue identifier="ABA-238">
<title>drain-cycle: plugins (entire.io) don't load headless — capture and document the divergence</title>
<description>
## Problem

The operator noticed some normal plugins (e.g. [entire.io](<http://entire.io>)'s checkpointing) didn't take effect during the headless drain, hinting the spawn environment diverges from an interactive session more than intended. A verification spike showed hooks *do* fire under `claude -p` (entire's SessionStart hook fired when run from a normal repo root) — so the likely difference is the worker's **worktree cwd** (`.worktrees/<id>` of the target repo) and/or where entire's hook is registered. Root cause needs runtime confirmation, not a guess.

## Change (observability-first — no blind fix)

* Add an opt-in `--debug-file <path>` to the worker spawn, written next to the run log, so the next run records exactly which plugins / MCP servers / hooks initialised.
* Add a documented one-shot reproduction step to `docs/design-decisions.md`: run `claude -p` headless in a worktree vs a repo root and diff the loaded plugins/hooks.
* Add a design-decision entry naming the headless-vs-interactive divergence as a known constraint, with the open question and the spike evidence (hooks fire under `-p`; the suspect is worktree cwd).

No code change to plugin/hook loading this round.

## Acceptance criteria

* `--debug-file` capture is wired into the spawn (opt-in) and lands beside the run log.
* `docs/design-decisions.md` has the reproduction step + divergence decision entry.
* A reproduction run confirms (or refutes) the worktree-cwd hypothesis and the finding is recorded.

## Recommended worker model

**Opus** — investigation/judgment-heavy, low code.

## Files

`drain_cycle/worker.py` (opt-in `--debug-file`), `docs/design-decisions.md` (reproduction + decision), `README.md` (debug capture note).

Depends on the streamed worker (<issue id="eb62c999-a798-4055-9d3c-7993542fbdf8">ABA-236</issue>).
</description>

1	"""Spawn a streaming ``claude -p`` worker for one issue and record its usage.
2	
3	The orchestrator owns the cycle loop and the Linear lifecycle; this module
4	owns one spawned session: how it is launched, how its token usage is
5	parsed off the wire, and how it is force-terminated when it overruns the
6	per-issue time cap.
7	
8	**Why stream-json.** A bare ``claude -p`` prints only the final assistant
9	text; the run log then records exit code and wall-clock and nothing about
10	spend. ``--output-format stream-json`` (requires ``--verbose``) emits one
11	JSON event per line — per-turn ``assistant`` messages plus a terminal
12	``result`` — from which we reconstruct exactly what the session cost.
13	
14	**Token accounting.** Each ``assistant`` event carries ``message.usage``,
15	but the same assistant message is emitted *once per content block*
16	(thinking, text, tool_use) — every copy repeats the identical usage under
17	the identical ``message.id``. Summing per event therefore double- (or
18	triple-) counts a turn; we key by ``message.id`` so each turn's usage is
19	counted exactly once. ``cumulative`` is the sum across turns of all four
20	token components — the real billed-token figure, dominated in long
21	tool-use loops by cache reads re-paid every turn. ``peak_context`` is the
22	largest single-turn context (input + cache_read + cache_create), i.e. how
23	close the session came to the context-window ceiling.
24	
25	The terminal ``result`` event is authoritative for ``cost_usd``
26	(``total_cost_usd``), ``num_turns``, ``session_id`` and ``is_error``. Note
27	``result.usage`` is only the *final* turn's snapshot, not the session
28	total, so it is deliberately not used for the token figures — those come
29	from the accumulated per-turn stream, which is also what survives a kill
30	before any ``result`` arrives.
31	
32	**Guardrails — belt and suspenders.** ``per_issue_cost_usd`` is handed to
33	``claude`` as ``--max-budget-usd`` so the session self-terminates on cost
34	(the native belt). The token and time caps are the orchestrator's
35	suspenders: a poll loop watches the live cumulative-token tally and the
36	elapsed wall-clock, and SIGKILLs the session the moment either is crossed.
37	Any cap passed as ``None`` is not enforced; with both token and time caps
38	off the worker simply waits for the session to exit on its own. A crossed
39	cap is reported back as a ``Breach`` so the orchestrator can name it in the
40	halt entry. See ``limits.py`` for the thresholds and their precedence.
41	
42	**Process-group kill.** The worker is launched with
43	``start_new_session=True`` so it leads its own process group. On a breach
44	the whole group is SIGKILLed via ``os.killpg``, reaping grandchildren —
45	MCP servers, sub-agents — that a kill of the direct child alone would
46	orphan to keep burning tokens. SIGKILL (not a SIGTERM grace) is used
47	deliberately: a session being force-terminated past a guardrail has no
48	clean-shutdown work worth waiting for, and SIGKILL is the only signal a
49	misbehaving grandchild cannot ignore. Killing the whole group also closes
50	the stdout pipe those grandchildren inherited, which is what lets the
51	reader thread reach EOF instead of blocking forever.
52	"""
53	from __future__ import annotations
54	
55	import json
56	import os
57	import signal
58	import subprocess
59	import sys
60	import threading
61	import time
62	from dataclasses import dataclass
63	from pathlib import Path
64	from typing import Any, TextIO
65	
66	from .limits import Breach
67	
68	_STREAM_FLAGS = ["--verbose", "--output-format", "stream-json"]
69	"""Flags that switch ``claude -p`` into line-delimited JSON event mode.
70	``--verbose`` is required: without it ``stream-json`` emits only the
71	terminal ``result`` event, dropping the per-turn ``assistant`` usage we
72	accumulate."""
73	
74	_READER_JOIN_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 5.0
75	"""Bound on waiting for the stdout-reader thread to drain to EOF after the
76	process exits. The group SIGKILL closes every inherited write-end of the
77	pipe, so EOF normally arrives at once; the bound only guards the
78	pathological case of a group member that somehow escaped the kill, so the
79	worker returns with the usage it has rather than hanging."""
80	
81	_POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS = 1.0
82	"""How often the breach monitor wakes to compare the live token tally and
83	elapsed wall-clock against the per-issue caps. A worry-free overshoot of at
84	most one interval before the kill lands; one second is responsive enough for
85	a session measured in minutes and cheap enough to poll."""
86	
87	_TOKEN_FIELDS = (
88	    "input_tokens",
89	    "output_tokens",
90	    "cache_creation_input_tokens",
91	    "cache_read_input_tokens",
92	)
93	
94	
95	@dataclass
96	class WorkerResult:
97	    """Outcome of one spawned session, recorded into the run-log entry.
98	
99	    ``exit_code`` is the process return code (negative when killed by a
100	    signal, e.g. ``-9`` on a guardrail SIGKILL). ``breach`` is the branch
101	    selector the orchestrator reads: ``None`` when the session ran to its
102	    own exit, otherwise the per-issue token or time cap that was crossed,
103	    carrying the cap and the value observed at kill time. ``usage`` carries
104	    the four token components plus ``cumulative`` and ``peak_context``.
105	    ``cost_usd`` / ``num_turns`` / ``session_id`` / ``is_error`` come from
106	    the terminal ``result`` event and are ``None`` when the session was
107	    killed before one arrived.
108	    """
109	
110	    exit_code: int
111	    breach: Breach | None
112	    duration_seconds: float
113	    model: str
114	    usage: dict[str, int]
115	    cost_usd: float | None
116	    num_turns: int | None
117	    session_id: str | None
118	    is_error: bool | None
119	
120	
121	def run_issue(
122	    *,
123	    claude_cmd: list[str],
124	    model: str,
125	    prompt: str,
126	    cwd: Path,
127	    token_limit: int | None,
128	    time_limit_seconds: float | None,
129	    cost_limit_usd: float | None,
130	    passthrough: TextIO | None = None,
131	    poll_interval_seconds: float = _POLL_INTERVAL_SECONDS,
132	) -> WorkerResult:
133	    """Spawn one streaming ``claude -p`` session and return its usage.
134	
135	    ``claude_cmd`` is the base argv (``["claude", "-p",
136	    "--dangerously-skip-permissions"]``); the streaming flags, ``--model``,
137	    ``--max-budget-usd`` (when ``cost_limit_usd`` is set), and the prompt are
138	    appended here. The session runs in its own process group; if its
139	    cumulative tokens cross ``token_limit`` or its wall-clock crosses
140	    ``time_limit_seconds`` the whole group is killed and the returned
141	    ``WorkerResult.breach`` names the cap. A limit passed as ``None`` is not
142	    enforced. ``cost_limit_usd`` is enforced by ``claude`` itself, not here.
143	
144	    Non-JSON lines on the merged stdout/stderr stream (claude warnings,
145	    hook stderr) are written to ``passthrough`` (default ``sys.stderr``) so
146	    the operator still sees diagnostics; recognised JSON events are
147	    consumed by the usage parser.
148	    """
149	    # The prompt is the trailing positional; it must follow the value-taking
150	    # ``--model`` / ``--max-budget-usd`` options, not sit between an option
151	    # and its value.
152	    argv = [*claude_cmd, *_STREAM_FLAGS, "--model", model]
153	    if cost_limit_usd is not None:
154	        argv += ["--max-budget-usd", f"{cost_limit_usd:g}"]
155	    argv.append(prompt)
156	    sink = passthrough if passthrough is not None else sys.stderr
157	    accumulator = _UsageAccumulator()
158	
159	    started = time.monotonic()
160	    proc = subprocess.Popen(
161	        argv,
162	        cwd=str(cwd),
163	        stdout=subprocess.PIPE,
164	        stderr=subprocess.STDOUT,
165	        text=True,
166	        bufsize=1,
167	        start_new_session=True,
168	    )
169	    reader = threading.Thread(
170	        target=_drain_stream,
171	        args=(proc.stdout, accumulator, sink),
172	        daemon=True,
173	    )
174	    reader.start()
175	
176	    breach = _monitor(
177	        proc,
178	        accumulator,
179	        started=started,
180	        token_limit=token_limit,
181	        time_limit_seconds=time_limit_seconds,
182	        poll_interval_seconds=poll_interval_seconds,
183	    )
184	    if breach is not None:
185	        _kill_process_group(proc)
186	        proc.wait()
187	    reader.join(timeout=_READER_JOIN_TIMEOUT_SECONDS)
188	    duration = time.monotonic() - started
189	
190	    cost_usd, num_turns, session_id, is_error = accumulator.summary()
191	    return WorkerResult(
192	        exit_code=proc.returncode,
193	        breach=breach,
194	        duration_seconds=duration,
195	        model=model,
196	        usage=accumulator.usage(),
197	        cost_usd=cost_usd,
198	        num_turns=num_turns,
199	        session_id=session_id,
200	        is_error=is_error,
201	    )
202	
203	
204	def _monitor(
205	    proc: subprocess.Popen[str],
206	    accumulator: _UsageAccumulator,
207	    *,
208	    started: float,
209	    token_limit: int | None,
210	    time_limit_seconds: float | None,
211	    poll_interval_seconds: float,
212	) -> Breach | None:
213	    """Wait for the session, returning the per-issue cap it breaches (if any).
214	
215	    With both caps off there is nothing to watch for, so we block on the
216	    process directly. Otherwise we wake every ``poll_interval_seconds`` to
217	    compare the live cumulative-token tally and elapsed wall-clock against
218	    the caps; the first crossed cap is returned for the caller to kill on.
219	    Returns ``None`` when the session exits on its own first.
220	    """
221	    if token_limit is None and time_limit_seconds is None:
222	        proc.wait()
223	        return None
224	    while True:
225	        try:
226	            proc.wait(timeout=poll_interval_seconds)
227	            return None
228	        except subprocess.TimeoutExpired:
229	            pass
230	        if token_limit is not None:
231	            observed = accumulator.cumulative()
232	            if observed >= token_limit:
233	                return Breach("per-issue", "token", token_limit, observed)
234	        if time_limit_seconds is not None:
235	            elapsed = time.monotonic() - started
236	            if elapsed >= time_limit_seconds:
237	                return Breach("per-issue", "time", time_limit_seconds, elapsed)
238	
239	
240	def _kill_process_group(proc: subprocess.Popen[str]) -> None:
241	    """SIGKILL the worker's whole process group, reaping grandchildren.
242	
243	    No-ops if the leader has already exited (its pid — and thus the group
244	    id — is gone). SIGKILL is sent before the caller reaps the leader, so
245	    the group still exists and its id cannot have been reused.
246	    """
247	    try:
248	        pgid = os.getpgid(proc.pid)
249	    except ProcessLookupError:
250	        return
251	    try:
252	        os.killpg(pgid, signal.SIGKILL)
253	    except ProcessLookupError:
254	        pass
255	
256	
257	def _drain_stream(
258	    stream: TextIO | None, accumulator: _UsageAccumulator, sink: TextIO
259	) -> None:
260	    """Read the worker's merged output line by line until EOF.
261	
262	    Recognised JSON object events feed the usage accumulator; everything
263	    else (non-JSON diagnostics, JSON that isn't an object) is echoed to
264	    ``sink`` so the operator keeps the visibility a captured pipe would
265	    otherwise hide.
266	    """
267	    if stream is None:
268	        return
269	    try:
270	        for line in stream:
271	            line = line.rstrip("\n")
272	            if not line:
273	                continue
274	            try:
275	                event = json.loads(line)
276	            except json.JSONDecodeError:
277	                _echo(sink, line)
278	                continue
279	            if isinstance(event, dict):
280	                accumulator.feed(event)
281	            else:
282	                _echo(sink, line)
283	    finally:
284	        try:
285	            stream.close()
286	        except OSError:
287	            pass
288	
289	
290	def _echo(sink: TextIO, line: str) -> None:
291	    try:
292	        print(line, file=sink)
293	    except (OSError, ValueError):
294	        pass
295	
296	
297	def _coerce_int(value: Any) -> int:
298	    return value if isinstance(value, int) else 0
299	
300	
301	class _UsageAccumulator:
302	    """Reconstruct session usage from the streamed events.
303	
304	    Per-turn usage is keyed by ``message.id`` so a turn emitted once per
305	    content block is counted once; the last copy wins (later events carry
306	    the more complete snapshot). The ``result`` event overwrites the
307	    session-summary fields each time it is seen.
308	
309	    All mutation happens on the reader thread and all reads happen on the
310	    main thread after it is joined — but the join is bounded, so a group
311	    member that escaped the kill (e.g. via ``setsid``) could keep the
312	    reader alive past the join. The lock makes the concurrent case safe
313	    rather than a ``dictionary changed size during iteration`` crash.
314	    """
315	
316	    def __init__(self) -> None:
317	        self._lock = threading.Lock()
318	        self._turns: dict[str, dict[str, int]] = {}
319	        self._anon = 0
320	        self.cost_usd: float | None = None
321	        self.num_turns: int | None = None
322	        self.session_id: str | None = None
323	        self.is_error: bool | None = None
324	
325	    def feed(self, event: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
326	        event_type = event.get("type")
327	        if event_type == "assistant":
328	            self._feed_assistant(event)
329	        elif event_type == "result":
330	            self._feed_result(event)
331	
332	    def _feed_assistant(self, event: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
333	        message = event.get("message")
334	        if not isinstance(message, dict):
335	            return
336	        usage = message.get("usage")
337	        if not isinstance(usage, dict):
338	            return
339	        message_id = message.get("id")
340	        turn = {field: _coerce_int(usage.get(field)) for field in _TOKEN_FIELDS}
341	        with self._lock:
342	            if isinstance(message_id, str):
343	                key = message_id
344	            else:
345	                key = f"_anon-{self._anon}"
346	                self._anon += 1
347	            self._turns[key] = turn
348	
349	    def _feed_result(self, event: dict[str, Any]) -> None:
350	        with self._lock:
351	            self.cost_usd = event.get("total_cost_usd")
352	            self.num_turns = event.get("num_turns")
353	            self.session_id = event.get("session_id")
354	            self.is_error = event.get("is_error")
355	
356	    def cumulative(self) -> int:
357	        """Live billed-token total across turns — the per-issue token cap is
358	        compared against this on every poll, so it reads under the lock and
359	        stays cheap (no peak-context / per-component breakdown)."""
360	        with self._lock:
361	            turns = list(self._turns.values())
362	        return sum(turn[field] for turn in turns for field in _TOKEN_FIELDS)
363	
364	    def usage(self) -> dict[str, int]:
365	        totals = {field: 0 for field in _TOKEN_FIELDS}
366	        peak_context = 0
367	        with self._lock:
368	            turns = list(self._turns.values())
369	        for turn in turns:
370	            for field in _TOKEN_FIELDS:
371	                totals[field] += turn[field]
372	            context = (
373	                turn["input_tokens"]
374	                + turn["cache_read_input_tokens"]
375	                + turn["cache_creation_input_tokens"]
376	            )
377	            peak_context = max(peak_context, context)
378	        return {
379	            **totals,
380	            "cumulative": sum(totals.values()),
381	            "peak_context": peak_context,
382	        }
383	
384	    def summary(self) -> tuple[float | None, int | None, str | None, bool | None]:
385	        """Read the result-event fields under the lock, as a snapshot."""
386	        with self._lock:
387	            return self.cost_usd, self.num_turns, self.session_id, self.is_error
388	
```

1	# Design decisions
2	
3	Design rationale for `drain-cycle`. Read this before making architectural changes — `AGENTS.md` points here.
4	
5	Nine decisions are recorded so a future reader doesn't have to reverse-engineer them from the code. ADRs would be heavier than this tool needs.
6	
7	## 1. The spawned agent updates Linear itself
8	
9	The orchestrator does **not** poll Linear and write status. The spawned `claude -p` session is told, in its prompt, to move its issue to Done on completion. The orchestrator only reads Linear after the session exits, to decide whether to advance or halt.
10	
11	**Alternative considered.** Orchestrator-owned status: the parent polls Linear, transitions states, owns the lifecycle. This is more conventional and easier to reason about.
12	
13	**Why the agent-self-update path.** The orchestrator can only observe *process exit*, not *task success*. A Claude session may exit 0 having done nothing useful, or exit non-zero having actually shipped — exit code is a poor proxy for "the issue is Done." Letting the agent assert Done in Linear forces it to make an explicit, observable claim about its own outcome, which is exactly the artefact we need to grade KR1 and trigger the kill condition. If this pattern proves unreliable, that's the initiative's kill condition firing — not a bug to paper over.
14	
15	## 2. `--dangerously-skip-permissions` is accepted
16	
17	Every spawned session runs with `--dangerously-skip-permissions`. The agent can run any tool on any path inside its worktree, including shell commands, file writes, and network calls, with no operator approval.
18	
19	**Blast radius.** Bounded to the per-issue worktree under `.worktrees/<issue-identifier>/` inside the target repo, plus the operator's Linear account (the agent can transition issues), plus whatever the spawned shell can reach (env vars, secrets in `~/.config`, network egress). Not bounded to the worktree at the filesystem level — a determined or confused agent can `cd` out.
20	
21	**Why accepted.** The single-operator personal-product context: target repos are mine, the Linear workspace is mine, the machine is mine. The point of the tool is removing prompts; gating them defeats the purpose. The mitigation is **scope discipline at cycle planning** — don't drain a cycle whose issues touch credentials, production systems, or shared infrastructure. This is operator responsibility, not a tool guarantee.
22	
23	## 3. Fresh worktree per issue, not a shared workspace
24	
25	Each issue gets `.worktrees/<issue-identifier>/` branched off `main`, used once, then either removed (on Done) or preserved (on halt).
26	
27	**Alternative considered.** Shared workspace where all issues run in the target repo's main checkout in sequence. Simpler, faster, no worktree plumbing.
28	
29	**Why worktree-per-issue.** Issues drift. An agent that misunderstands its task can leave the workspace in a broken state — half-applied edits, uncommitted files, branch in the wrong place — that contaminates every subsequent issue's starting point. The worktree gives each issue a clean, identical starting point regardless of what the previous one did, and preservation-on-halt (US-B) means inspectable debug state. The cost is filesystem space and a few seconds of branch setup per issue. Cheap.
30	
31	## 4. Run-log is one file per invocation, not one file per cycle
32	
33	Each `drain-cycle` invocation writes its own run-log file at `~/.drain-cycle/runs/<cycle-id>-<run-timestamp>.json`. The per-file schema is unchanged — `{cycle_id, cycle_duration_seconds, entries: [...]}` — and `cycle_id` inside each file is how downstream readers group runs of the same cycle.
34	
35	**Alternatives considered.** (A) Multi-run schema in one file: `{cycle_id, runs: [...]}`. Faithful, but every reader has to learn the new shape and the on-disk backup file needs migrating. (B) Open-and-extend: load the existing file and append to a single flat `entries` list. Loses run boundaries, and `cycle_duration_seconds` (computed as `max(finished_at) - min(started_at)`) spans the inter-run gap and becomes misleading. (D) Refuse to clobber: fail-fast if the file exists. Breaks the unattended re-run flow the tool exists for (fix X, re-run, drain the rest) until a resume mode is built.
36	
37	**Why per-run files.** The bug being fixed (ABA-230) is that a second invocation against the same cycle silently overwrites the first run's data. Per-run files make the write path single-writer-write-once — no read-then-write race, no schema diff, no migration. US-D (ABA-197), which already plans to glob `runs/*.json` and merge across cycles, gets a one-line addition (group by `cycle_id`) instead of a new shape to read. Each file's `cycle_duration_seconds` represents one invocation's hands-off time; US-D sums them when reporting cycle-level KR2.
38	
39	**Cost.** A re-run cycle accumulates one file per invocation. At cycle scale (≤ 15 issues, rarely more than 2–3 invocations to drain) this is negligible. No retention policy is shipped; trim by hand if it ever matters.
40	
41	## 5. Each issue declares its target repo via a `repo:<name>` Linear label
42	
43	The orchestrator used to be single-repo by construction: `repo = Path.cwd()`, every worktree under `<cwd>/.worktrees/`. Cycles in this workspace span multiple repos by design — `linear-workflow.md` makes "Affected repos" part of the six initiative-readiness fields, and the Ops slot deliberately holds cross-repo maintenance issues. Each issue now carries a `repo:<name>` Linear label; `~/.drain-cycle/repos.yml` maps the name to an absolute path; the operator runs `drain-cycle` from anywhere.
44	
45	**Alternatives considered.**
46	
47	- *Description-body encoding* (e.g. a `Repo: drain-cycle` line in the markdown). The description is the most-edited surface, agents rewrite it routinely, and there's no schema enforcement. A label is one structured field with one value — Linear validates it, and a label rename triggers a clear "this label doesn't exist" error rather than silently drifting to a wrong repo.
48	- *Title prefix* (e.g. `[drain-cycle] Fix the foo`). Cleaner machine parse than the body, but every issue's title gains visual clutter for a parser-only concern. Labels don't pay that cost.
49	- *Inherit from the Linear project's "affected repos"*. Ambiguous for projects that legitimately touch multiple repos, and there is no obvious answer for the Ops container project, which is multi-repo by definition.
50	
51	**Missing-repo halt behaviour.** Same machinery as every other pre-spawn halt: write a run-log entry, print the `Halt:` line to stderr, exit 1, leave subsequent issues untouched. The new wrinkle is that resolution failures happen before any Linear state is moved, so they skip the post-spawn revert path (`_revert_to_pre_halt_state`). This is the difference from the existing setup-failure halt: that one happens after `worktree.add` or the initial `linear.set_state` attempt; the resolution halt happens before either. Either way, no revert is needed because no state was moved.
52	
53	**`repos.yml` config errors are even earlier.** A missing or malformed `repos.yml` halts the CLI at startup, before any Linear traffic and before the run-log file is created. There is no cycle yet to log against, so the failure surfaces only on stderr. This is enforced eagerly in `cli.main` so the orchestrator never sees a broken config.
54	
55	**Why labels over file conventions.** (e.g. requiring the operator to put the issue identifier in a branch comment, or use a remote-name convention). Labels are the only signal that's enforceable in Linear's UI: cycle planners can see at a glance which repo an issue targets, and the multi-repo distinction is visible at the right surface (Linear) rather than buried in a config file.
56	
57	**Out-of-v1 deliberately.** No env-var expansion inside `repos.yml`; no auto-clone if the path is missing; no parallelism across repos; no retroactive labelling of pre-cycle issues. All are operator-time concerns rather than tool-time concerns. Multi-team Linear support stays out of scope too — the tool is still hardcoded to the `Personal` team.
58	
59	## 6. Installed as a `uv tool`, with the secret read from `~/.drain-cycle/.env`
60	
61	`drain-cycle` is installed via `uv tool install`, which puts the executable on `$PATH` in an isolated environment. The Linear API key is read from `~/.drain-cycle/.env` (shell-exported vars still win), beside the `repos.yml` config and the `runs/` logs the tool already kept there.
62	
63	**Alternatives considered.**
64	
65	- *`pipx`*. Functionally equivalent for installing a Python CLI in isolation. Rejected because `uv` already anchors this repo's stack (`uv.lock`, the `mise.toml` Python pin) — adding `pipx` spends an innovation token on a second tool that does the same job.
66	- *Publish to PyPI*. Lets anyone `uv tool install drain-cycle` by name, but buys a release-and-versioning burden — tagging, changelogs, a name on the index — that a single-operator tool doesn't earn. `uv tool install git+https://…` already covers install-from-anywhere with no release step.
67	
68	**The secret-loading change this forced.** The CLI used to load `.env` only from the repo root (`Path(__file__).parent.parent`). Once installed, the package lives in the isolated tool env, where that path has no `.env` — so the key has to live somewhere stable. The load order is now shell env → `~/.drain-cycle/.env` → repo-root `.env`, first hit wins (`load_dotenv` defaults to `override=False`). The repo-root entry survives only as a dev-checkout fallback (it works under `--editable`, where `__file__` still points into the checkout); the installed tool reads the key from `~/.drain-cycle/.env`.
69	
70	**Out-of-v1 deliberately.** No PyPI release. No `drain-cycle init` command to scaffold `~/.drain-cycle/` — a missing `repos.yml` already halts with an actionable message that prints the expected shape, and `docs/repos.example.yml` is a copyable template, which is enough for a single operator.
71	
72	## 7. Workers default to Sonnet; a `model:` label overrides per issue
73	
74	A spawned `claude -p` worker inherits whatever model the operator has globally pinned. In the diagnosed quota-burn run all five workers ran on `claude-opus-4-7` (the operator's global pin), and Opus was the single largest cost multiplier of the ~108M-token spend. Workers now default to `claude-sonnet-4-6`, passed explicitly via `--model`; an individual issue opts up (or down) with a `model:<alias>` Linear label, mirroring the `repo:<name>` mechanism. Known aliases (`sonnet`/`opus`/`haiku`) map to full ids; an unrecognised value is passed to `claude --model` verbatim.
75	
76	**Why lenient, not strict.** Unlike `repo:` resolution — where a missing label is a hard halt because there is no safe default target — model resolution always has a safe fallback. So it never raises: no label, an unknown alias, or conflicting `model:` labels all fall back to the default rather than halting an unattended cycle over a label typo. The model actually used is recorded in the run log, so a mis-labelled issue surfaces after the fact instead of stalling the run.
77	
78	**Alternatives considered.** (A) Keep inheriting the global pin — rejected, it is exactly what caused the burn and gives the operator no per-issue control. (B) A single global `--model` flag with no per-issue override — simpler, but a cycle legitimately mixes cheap mechanical issues with a few that warrant Opus; per-issue is the right grain. (C) Raise on ambiguous labels like `repo:` does — rejected, halting a whole unattended cycle over a duplicate label is worse than silently taking the cheap, safe default.
79	
80	## 8. Workers use stream-json output; usage is parsed from the wire and the worker leads its own process group
81	
82	The worker launches with `claude -p --verbose --output-format stream-json` via `subprocess.Popen(..., stdout=PIPE, stderr=STDOUT, text=True, bufsize=1, start_new_session=True)`, reading output line by line in a reader thread. The per-issue run-log entry gains `model`, a `usage` block (the four token components + `cumulative` + `peak_context`), `cost_usd`, `num_turns`, `session_id`, `is_error`, and an explicit `duration_seconds`; the file gains top-level `cycle_cost_usd` and `cycle_tokens_cumulative`. All of this lives in `drain_cycle/worker.py`; the orchestrator calls `worker.run_issue(...)` and records the result.
83	
84	**The problem.** A run that burned ~108M tokens left the operator with no on-disk record of *which issue* spent what — usage had to be reconstructed from `~/.claude/projects/*.jsonl`. Spend is the metric the cost guardrail (a downstream slice) acts on, so it has to be captured at the source.
85	
86	**Why parse the stream rather than the JSONL transcript.** The transcript files are keyed by session and live outside the worktree; correlating them back to an issue after the fact is exactly the manual step this removes. The event stream is emitted by the process we already spawn, in real time, and carries everything we need.
87	
88	**Token accounting — dedup by `message.id`.** The same `assistant` message is emitted *once per content block* (thinking, text, tool_use), each copy repeating the identical `message.usage`. Summing per event double-counts a turn, so usage is keyed by `message.id` and counted once. `cumulative` sums all four token components across turns — the real billed total, dominated in long tool-use loops by cache reads re-paid every turn (this is what the 108M figure was). `result.usage` is deliberately *not* used for the totals: it is only the final turn's snapshot, and it is absent entirely when a session is killed before finishing. The terminal `result` event is authoritative only for `cost_usd` (`total_cost_usd`), `num_turns`, `session_id`, `is_error`; those are `null` on a killed session.
89	
90	**Why `start_new_session=True` + `os.killpg`.** The old `subprocess.run(timeout=)` killed only the direct child on timeout, orphaning grandchildren — MCP servers, sub-agents — that kept consuming. Making the worker a process-group leader and SIGKILLing the whole group on the time cap reaps them. SIGKILL with no SIGTERM grace is deliberate: a session past its deadline has no clean-shutdown work worth waiting for, and SIGKILL is the only signal a wedged grandchild cannot ignore. Killing the group also closes the stdout pipe those grandchildren inherited, which is what lets the reader thread reach EOF instead of blocking.
91	
92	**Additive schema.** `grade.py` reads only `cycle_id` and `entries[].final_linear_state` / `exit_code`, so the new fields don't touch grading and pre-existing run logs grade unchanged. Entries written before any session runs (resolution and setup-failure halts) carry `null` for the worker fields but keep the same key set.
93	
94	- **Parallelism.** Issues run one at a time. The Linear cycle is the unit; intra-cycle parallelism adds resource contention and serialises poorly with the agent-self-update pattern (two agents racing to mark different issues Done is fine, but two agents racing on overlapping files is not).
95	- **Retry.** A halted issue is not retried automatically. The operator inspects the worktree and decides — fix, redo manually, or descope.
96	- **Cross-cycle scheduling.** One cycle per invocation. Chaining cycles is an operator concern.
97	
98	## 9. Resource guardrails: a native cost belt and orchestrator token/time suspenders
99	
100	Before this, the only ceiling on a worker was a 3600s wall-clock timeout, and the cycle as a whole had none. A single diagnosed run burned ~108M tokens across five issues with no circuit-breaker. `drain_cycle/limits.py` now defines per-issue and cycle-wide caps on tokens, wall-clock, and cost, enforced in two layers:
101	
102	- **Native belt.** The per-issue cost cap is passed to `claude` as `--max-budget-usd`, so the session self-terminates on spend without the orchestrator watching it.
103	- **Orchestrator suspenders.** The per-issue token and time caps are enforced by the worker against the live event stream: a poll loop compares the running cumulative-token tally and elapsed wall-clock against the caps and SIGKILLs the session's process group on the first breach (reusing the group-kill machinery from decision 8). The cycle-wide caps are enforced by the orchestrator between issues — after each Done issue it sums the run log's running totals and stops the run if any cycle cap is crossed.
104	
105	**Why both layers.** The cost belt is the cheapest possible enforcement — `claude` already meters its own spend — but it only knows about *this* session's dollars, and a subscription user cares about tokens, not dollars. The token cap is therefore the primary guardrail and has to be the orchestrator's job, since `claude` exposes no `--max-tokens` equivalent for a whole session. Time is enforced the same way because a session can wedge while emitting no usage at all (the old 3600s timeout's job), so wall-clock can't be inferred from the token stream.
106	
107	**Why the cycle caps live in the orchestrator, not the worker.** A worker only sees its own issue. The failure mode the cycle caps exist for is death-by-aggregate: every issue stays comfortably under its per-issue cap while their sum drains the quota (8M × 5 = 40M, past a 30M cycle cap, with no single issue ever breaching). Only the orchestrator, which holds the run log's running totals, can see that — so it checks after each Done issue and stops before spawning the next.
108	
109	**Defaults: per-issue 8M tokens · 20 min · $15; cycle 30M tokens · 90 min · $60.** These are deliberately generous starting points sized below the diagnosed bad run (one issue alone hit 43M tokens / 23 min), not tuned values. The intent is a circuit-breaker that trips on the pathological case, not a tight budget. They are meant to be recalibrated against real run-log spend.
110	
111	**Each guardrail is independently on/off-able.** Any cap can be `None` (off). The defaults are all live; an operator turns one off with `null` in the optional `~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml`. With both the per-issue token and time caps off, the worker simply waits for the session to exit on its own — there is no longer any implicit outer timeout, which is the operator's explicit choice when they disable both.
112	
113	**The time cap absorbed the old 3600s timeout.** Rather than keep a separate hardcoded outer timeout alongside the configurable time cap, the time cap *is* the timeout — `per_issue_seconds` (default 20 min, tighter than the old 3600s and below the diagnosed 23-min overrun). One time concept, configurable, instead of two.
114	
115	**Breach reporting.** A breach is a small `Breach(scope, metric, limit, observed)` value whose `describe()` renders the operator-facing line — used verbatim by the worker (per-issue kill) and the orchestrator (cycle stop) so the wording can't drift. A per-issue breach takes the existing exit-1 + revert + `halt_reason` contract, naming the cap and the value at kill time. A cycle breach lands in the run log's top-level `cycle_halt_reason`: the breaching issue's own entry is a normal Done, and the top-level field explains why the run stopped.
116	
117	**`limits.yml` semantics and validation.** Absent key → baked-in default; explicit `null` → guardrail off; positive number → override. A present-but-malformed file (unknown key, non-positive, non-numeric, bool, invalid YAML) raises `LimitsConfigError` and halts at CLI startup — mirroring the eager `repos.yml` validation (decision 5). The reasoning is sharper here: silently falling back to defaults on a typo would leave the operator believing a tighter cap was active when it wasn't, which is worse than a loud halt.
118	
119	**Alternatives considered.**
120	
121	- *CLI flags to override limits per invocation.* Deferred. The acceptance criteria require only defaults + `limits.yml`, and `cli.main` is a deliberately minimal exact-match dispatcher (decision in `cli.py`); adding an argument parser to thread per-run overrides is scope the single operator can cover by editing `limits.yml`. Revisit if a use case for one-off overrides appears.
122	- *Kill on the cycle cap mid-stream (pass cycle-so-far totals into the worker).* Rejected as unnecessary: the per-issue cap (8M) is below the cycle cap (30M), so a single issue can't cross the cycle cap before crossing its own; checking between issues catches the aggregate case without coupling the worker to cycle state.
123	- *A separate hardcoded outer timeout kept alongside the configurable caps.* Rejected — two time concepts where one suffices (see above).
124	
```

```
1	# drain-cycle
2	
3	Unattended execution of a Linear cycle. One invocation, no prompts; each issue runs in an isolated worktree under a fresh `claude -p` session that updates Linear itself.
4	
5	## Why this exists
6	
7	Today, executing a Linear cycle is manual: launch Claude per issue, watch it run, approve permissions, update Linear, repeat. Execution shepherding consumes time that should go to *scoping the next cycle* and *validating delivered work*. `drain-cycle` removes that shepherding so attention shifts back to scoping and validation. The full goal / KRs / kill condition live in the Linear project description.
8	
9	## Is this for you?
10	
11	Read this section before installing — `drain-cycle` is deliberately not for everyone.
12	
13	- **You need to be confident in your cycle planning.** `drain-cycle` executes the cycle you scoped — it does not reshape it. A poorly-scoped cycle drains faster than a manually-shepherded one — the multiplier works in both directions. Plan deliberately; this tool is the multiplier, not the safety net.
14	- **Accept the risk profile.** Every spawned session runs with `--dangerously-skip-permissions`. The agent can write files, run shell commands, hit the network, and update Linear without asking. The blast radius is documented in [`docs/design-decisions.md`](docs/design-decisions.md). If that makes you uncomfortable, that's the right instinct — stick with manual `claude` runs until it doesn't. Comfort with unattended execution is earned, not assumed.
15	- **Personal product, single operator.** One operator, one machine, one Linear workspace. Not designed for shared infra, production-touching cycles, or team workflows.
16	
17	## Prerequisites
18	
19	- **[`uv`](https://docs.astral.sh/uv/) on `$PATH`** — the installer; it also fetches the pinned Python for the tool's isolated env, so you don't install Python yourself.
20	- **`git` CLI** on `$PATH`.
21	- **`claude` CLI** on `$PATH` — see the [Claude Code install docs](https://docs.claude.com/en/docs/claude-code/setup).
22	- **Linear API key** with read/write on your Personal team. Generate one at <https://linear.app/settings/api>.
23	- **A `repo:<name>` Linear label on every cycle issue.** `drain-cycle` resolves the target repo per issue from this label — an unlabelled issue halts the run before any worktree is created. Create the labels at the team level in Linear (Settings → Labels) using the exact names your `repos.yml` keys use.
24	- **An optional `model:<alias>` Linear label.** Workers default to `claude-sonnet-4-6` — the cost-efficient default for unattended drains. Add `model:opus` (or `model:haiku`, or a full model id like `model:claude-opus-4-7`) to an issue that warrants a different model; that issue's worker runs on it. Missing, unknown, or conflicting labels fall back to the default — model resolution never halts a run.
25	- **A `~/.drain-cycle/repos.yml` config file** mapping each `<name>` to the absolute path of the repo on disk (see Install below).
26	- **Add `.worktrees/` to each target repo's `.gitignore`.** `drain-cycle` creates `.worktrees/<issue-identifier>/` inside the target repo per spawned session; without an ignore rule the spawned `git add` calls can sweep those paths into commits. A one-line entry per repo is enough.
27	
28	## Install
29	
30	Install with `uv` so the `drain-cycle` executable lands on `$PATH` in an isolated environment — no venv to activate:
31	
32	```bash
33	uv tool install .                                          # from a local checkout
34	uv tool install git+https://github.com/ababushkin/drain-cycle  # or straight from the repo
35	```
36	
37	`uv` installs the executable under `~/.local/bin`. If that isn't on your `$PATH`, run `uv tool update-shell` once. The CLI resolves each issue's target repo from its `repo:<name>` label, so the run directory is irrelevant.
38	
39	Then set up `~/.drain-cycle/`, which holds everything the tool reads and writes (config, secret, run logs):
40	
41	```bash
42	mkdir -p ~/.drain-cycle
43	echo 'LINEAR_API_KEY=lin_api_…' > ~/.drain-cycle/.env
44	```
45	
46	The CLI reads the key from the first source that defines it: a shell-exported `LINEAR_API_KEY` wins, then `~/.drain-cycle/.env`, then a `.env` at the repo root (a dev-checkout fallback the installed tool never sees). Export it in your shell rc instead of the file if you prefer.
47	
48	Create `~/.drain-cycle/repos.yml` mapping each label name to the repo's absolute path on disk:
49	
50	```yaml
51	repos:
52	  drain-cycle:  /Users/you/src/drain-cycle
53	  pde-skills:   /Users/you/src/pde-skills
54	  stock-review: /Users/you/src/stock-review
55	```
56	
57	Paths starting with `~` expand against `$HOME`. A missing or malformed `repos.yml` halts the CLI before any Linear traffic — exit 1, message on stderr, no run-log entry written. See [`docs/repos.example.yml`](docs/repos.example.yml) for a copyable template.
58	
59	Verify the install from anywhere:
60	
61	```bash
62	cd /tmp && drain-cycle --help     # any invocation that doesn't crash on import works
63	```
64	
65	### Developing on drain-cycle
66	
67	Working on the tool itself? Pin the dev Python and install editable so source edits take effect without reinstalling:
68	
69	```bash
70	mise install                       # picks up mise.toml → Python 3.12
71	uv tool install --editable .       # symlinks the checkout
72	uv tool install --reinstall .      # or rebuild from scratch after a change
73	```
74	
75	## Usage
76	
77	```
78	drain-cycle
79	```
80	
81	Run from anywhere — `drain-cycle` resolves each issue's target repo from its `repo:<name>` label. Drains the current cycle's Todo/Backlog issues (in priority order) until either the cycle is empty (exit 0) or an issue halts (exit 1).
82	
83	### What a run looks like
84	
85	```bash
86	$ drain-cycle
87	
88	# drain-cycle reads the current Linear cycle, fetches its Todo/Backlog issues,
89	# and works through them one at a time. Each issue's target repo is resolved
90	# from its `repo:<name>` label against ~/.drain-cycle/repos.yml.
91	
92	drain-cycle: picked ABA-241: Persist halt_reason in run-log entries
93	
94	# ABA-241 is labelled `repo:drain-cycle`, which `repos.yml` maps to
95	# ~/src/drain-cycle. The orchestrator creates a fresh git worktree under
96	# ~/src/drain-cycle/.worktrees/ABA-241/ branched off main, flips the Linear
97	# issue to In Progress, then spawns a `claude -p` session inside the worktree.
98	
99	  (… claude -p session does the work: reads the issue, edits files,
100	       runs tests, commits, pushes, marks the issue Done in Linear …)
101	
102	drain-cycle: ABA-241 done; worktree removed.
103	drain-cycle: picked PDE-12: Add the new triage skill
104	
105	# PDE-12 is labelled `repo:pde-skills`, so this run lands in a worktree
106	# under ~/src/pde-skills/.worktrees/PDE-12/ — different repo, same machinery.
107	
108	  (… second session runs …)
109	
110	drain-cycle: PDE-12 done; worktree removed.
111	drain-cycle: picked ABA-243: Compute cycle_duration_seconds
112	
113	  (… third session runs, but doesn't finish the work …)
114	
115	# The session exited but the Linear issue is still In Progress — meaning the
116	# agent didn't mark it Done. drain-cycle stops the run immediately so you can
117	# investigate. The worktree is left in place; the run log captures the same
118	# halt line you see on screen.
119	
120	Halt: ABA-243 (final state: In Progress) at /Users/you/src/drain-cycle/.worktrees/ABA-243
121	$ echo $?
122	1
123	```
124	
125	After the run, one JSON log per invocation is at `~/.drain-cycle/runs/<cycle-id>-<run-timestamp>.json`. On halt, the worktree at `<target-repo>/.worktrees/<issue-identifier>/` is preserved for inspection; on success it's removed.
126	
127	## Recommended companion skills
128	
129	Spawned sessions use whatever skills you've installed globally. The pairing it was designed for:
130	
131	- [**`ababushkin/pde-skills`**](https://github.com/ababushkin/pde-skills) — planning + engineering skill pack. Use it to *shape* the cycle (initiative, KRs, slices) before draining it. `drain-cycle` only multiplies execution; the quality of the cycle is upstream of this tool.
132	- [**`addyosmani/agent-skills`**](https://github.com/addyosmani/agent-skills) — Addy Osmani's pack covers complementary build / test / review skills the spawned sessions lean on.
133	
134	Both are skill packs for Claude Code — install them globally and the spawned `claude -p` sessions will pick them up automatically.
135	
136	## Limits
137	
138	Each worker is bounded by resource guardrails so a runaway session — or a cycle of merely expensive ones — can't drain your whole quota unattended. There are two layers:
139	
140	- **Native belt** — the per-issue cost cap is handed to `claude` as `--max-budget-usd`, so the session self-terminates on spend.
141	- **Orchestrator suspenders** — the per-issue token and time caps are enforced by killing the worker's whole process group the moment either is crossed; the cycle-wide caps (tokens, cost, wall-clock) stop the run between issues when their running total is breached. A per-issue breach lands in the halting entry's `halt_reason`; a cycle breach is recorded in the run log's top-level `cycle_halt_reason`.
142	
143	Token count is the primary guardrail (a subscription user pays in tokens, not dollars); cost rides alongside it.
144	
145	Defaults are baked in — **per-issue 8M tokens · 20 min · $15; cycle 30M tokens · 90 min · $60** — and live with no config. To change them, drop a `~/.drain-cycle/limits.yml` (optional) overriding only the caps you care about:
146	
147	```yaml
148	per_issue_tokens: 4000000   # tighten the per-issue token cap
149	cycle_cost_usd: null        # disable the cycle cost cap entirely
150	```
151	
152	A key you omit keeps its default; a number overrides it; `null` turns that guardrail off. Each value must be a positive number or `null` — a malformed file halts startup rather than silently reverting to defaults. See [`docs/limits.example.yml`](docs/limits.example.yml) for the full annotated template. The defaults are deliberately generous starting points; recalibrate against your real run-log spend.
153	
154	## Logs & grading
155	
156	Every invocation writes one JSON file: `~/.drain-cycle/runs/<cycle-id>-<run-timestamp>.json`. One entry per attempted issue, including timestamps, exit code, final Linear state, worktree path, per-issue token usage and cost, and `halt_reason` on the halting entry. The file also carries per-cycle totals (`cycle_tokens_cumulative`, `cycle_cost_usd`, `cycle_duration_seconds`) and a `cycle_halt_reason` when a cycle-wide cap stopped the run. Use it to gauge how cleanly your runs complete and what they cost — see [`drain_cycle/runlog.py`](drain_cycle/runlog.py) for the schema.
157	
158	## Design
159	
160	Design decisions, alternatives considered, and deliberate out-of-scope choices live in [`docs/design-decisions.md`](docs/design-decisions.md).
161	
```

1	"""Drain a cycle by iterating over its sorted Todo/Backlog issues.
2	
3	Halt-on-not-Done, the orchestrator-owned Todo→In-Progress transition, the
4	run-log artefact, and the inspectable-halt UX all live here. The halt-message
5	helper ``_halt_message`` is the single source of truth for the operator-facing
6	halt string — emitted both on stderr and into the run-log entry's
7	``halt_reason`` field.
8	"""
9	from __future__ import annotations
10	
11	import sys
12	from datetime import datetime, timezone
13	from pathlib import Path
14	
15	from . import linear, model, prompt, runlog, worker, worktree
16	from .limits import Limits, check_cycle
17	from .repos import RepoResolutionError, Repos
18	
19	_DONE_STATE_TYPE = "completed"
20	_IN_PROGRESS_STATE_NAME = "In Progress"
21	_CLAUDE_CMD = ["claude", "-p", "--dangerously-skip-permissions"]
22	_UNRESOLVED_WORKTREE_DISPLAY = "<unresolved>"
23	"""Worktree-path placeholder for the pre-spawn resolution-halt path.
24	No path has been chosen yet — the issue couldn't be mapped to a target
25	repo — so the run-log entry and stderr halt line carry this marker
26	rather than a misleading fake path."""
27	
28	
29	def _now_iso() -> str:
30	    return datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat()
31	
32	
33	def _halt_message(identifier: str, state_name: str, worktree_path: Path) -> str:
34	    """Single source of truth for the halt UX.
35	
36	    The same string lands on stderr (the operator's grep anchor) and in
37	    the run-log entry's ``halt_reason`` field — so kill-condition tooling
38	    reads the same human-readable explanation the operator saw at halt
39	    time.
40	    """
41	    return f"Halt: {identifier} (final state: {state_name}) at {worktree_path}"
42	
43	
44	def _revert_to_pre_halt_state(
45	    issue_id: str, *, target_state_name: str, pre_revert_state_name: str
46	) -> tuple[str, str | None]:
47	    """Restore Linear state on halt; return ``(state_to_report, error_msg)``.
48	
49	    The orchestrator transitions issues Todo→In Progress before spawning the
50	    agent. When the run halts, that In-Progress flag leaves the issue outside
51	    ``_PENDING_STATE_TYPES`` so a re-run silently skips it. This helper
52	    reverses the transition and re-fetches to confirm.
53	
54	    On revert success, returns the refreshed state name and ``None``.
55	    On revert failure, returns ``pre_revert_state_name`` (the state the
56	    issue is actually still in, so the operator can find it) plus the
57	    exception message — non-fatal. A failed refresh after a
58	    successful revert falls back to ``target_state_name``, since we trust
59	    the mutation landed even if the read-back didn't.
60	    """
61	    try:
62	        linear.set_state(issue_id, target_state_name)
63	    except Exception as exc:
64	        return pre_revert_state_name, str(exc)
65	    try:
66	        refreshed = linear.get_issue(issue_id)
67	    except Exception:
68	        return target_state_name, None
69	    return refreshed["state"]["name"], None
70	
71	
72	def _worker_log_fields(result: worker.WorkerResult) -> dict[str, object]:
73	    """Map a ``WorkerResult`` onto the run-log entry's usage fields.
74	
75	    Shared by all three worker-backed ``append_entry`` calls (timeout
76	    halt, Done, not-Done halt) so the recorded usage shape can't drift
77	    between branches.
78	    """
79	    return {
80	        "duration_seconds": result.duration_seconds,
81	        "model": result.model,
82	        "usage": result.usage,
83	        "cost_usd": result.cost_usd,
84	        "num_turns": result.num_turns,
85	        "session_id": result.session_id,
86	        "is_error": result.is_error,
87	    }
88	
89	
90	def run(repos: Repos, limits: Limits | None = None) -> int:
91	    if limits is None:
92	        limits = Limits()
93	    cycle_id = linear.current_cycle_id()
94	    log = runlog.RunLog(cycle_id=cycle_id)
95	    issues = linear.pending_issues(cycle_id)
96	    if not issues:
97	        print(f"Cycle {cycle_id} has no Todo/Backlog issues — nothing to do.")
98	        return 0
99	
100	    for issue in issues:
101	        identifier = issue["identifier"]
102	        print(f"drain-cycle: picked {identifier}: {issue['title']}", file=sys.stderr)
103	
104	        started_at = _now_iso()
105	        try:
106	            target_repo = repos.resolve(issue)
107	        except RepoResolutionError as exc:
108	            # Pre-spawn resolution halt: no Linear state was moved, so no
109	            # revert is attempted. The worktree path is the ``<unresolved>``
110	            # placeholder since no repo was chosen.
111	            state_name = issue["state"]["name"]
112	            halt_reason = (
113	                f"{_halt_message(identifier, state_name, Path(_UNRESOLVED_WORKTREE_DISPLAY))}"
114	                f" — {exc}"
115	            )
116	            log.append_entry(
117	                issue_identifier=identifier,
118	                started_at=started_at,
119	                finished_at=_now_iso(),
120	                exit_code=-1,
121	                final_linear_state=state_name,
122	                REDACTED,
123	                halt_reason=halt_reason,
124	            )
125	            print(halt_reason, file=sys.stderr)
126	            return 1
127	
128	        try:
129	            worktree_path = worktree.add(target_repo, identifier)
130	            # Orchestrator owns the Todo→In Progress half so the lifecycle
131	            # doesn't depend on the spawned agent's compliance. The agent
132	            # still owns the …→Done half via Linear MCP — see prompt.py tail.
133	            linear.set_state(issue["id"], _IN_PROGRESS_STATE_NAME)
134	        except Exception as exc:
135	            # Convert any pre-spawn failure into a recorded halt rather than
136	            # a traceback: write a run-log entry with the planned worktree
137	            # path, print the halt message, exit non-zero. Subsequent issues
138	            # are not attempted — same contract as a spawn-time halt.
139	            planned_path = target_repo / worktree.WORKTREE_DIR / identifier
140	            state_name = issue["state"]["name"]
141	            halt_reason = (
142	                f"{_halt_message(identifier, state_name, planned_path)}"
143	                f" — setup failed: {exc}"
144	            )
145	            log.append_entry(
146	                issue_identifier=identifier,
147	                started_at=started_at,
148	                finished_at=_now_iso(),
149	                exit_code=-1,
150	                final_linear_state=state_name,
151	                worktree_path=str(planned_path),
152	                halt_reason=halt_reason,
153	            )
154	            print(halt_reason, file=sys.stderr)
155	            return 1
156	
157	        agent_prompt = prompt.build(issue, worktree_path)
158	        worker_model = model.resolve(issue)
159	
160	        result = worker.run_issue(
161	            claude_cmd=_CLAUDE_CMD,
162	            model=worker_model,
163	            prompt=agent_prompt,
164	            cwd=worktree_path,
165	            token_limit=limits.per_issue_tokens,
166	            time_limit_seconds=limits.per_issue_seconds,
167	            cost_limit_usd=limits.per_issue_cost_usd,
168	        )
169	        finished_at = _now_iso()
170	
171	        if result.breach is not None:
172	            # The worker crossed a per-issue cap (tokens or time) and was
173	            # process-group killed (grandchildren reaped). Same revert + halt
174	            # contract as a not-Done exit, with the recorded usage of the
175	            # killed session and the breached cap named in the halt reason.
176	            original_state_name = issue["state"]["name"]
177	            effective_state, revert_error = _revert_to_pre_halt_state(
178	                issue["id"],
179	                target_state_name=original_state_name,
180	                pre_revert_state_name=_IN_PROGRESS_STATE_NAME,
181	            )
182	            halt_reason = (
183	                f"{_halt_message(identifier, effective_state, worktree_path)}"
184	                f" — {result.breach.describe()}"
185	            )
186	            if revert_error is not None:
187	                halt_reason += (
188	                    f"; revert to {original_state_name!r} failed: {revert_error}"
189	                )
190	            log.append_entry(
191	                issue_identifier=identifier,
192	                started_at=started_at,
193	                finished_at=finished_at,
194	                exit_code=result.exit_code,
195	                final_linear_state=effective_state,
196	                worktree_path=str(worktree_path),
197	                halt_reason=halt_reason,
198	                **_worker_log_fields(result),
199	            )
200	            print(halt_reason, file=sys.stderr)
201	            return 1
202	
203	        refreshed = linear.get_issue(issue["id"])
204	        post_spawn_state = refreshed["state"]["name"]
205	        is_done = refreshed["state"]["type"] == _DONE_STATE_TYPE
206	
207	        if is_done:
208	            # Append unconditionally for every attempted issue.
209	            log.append_entry(
210	                issue_identifier=identifier,
211	                started_at=started_at,
212	                finished_at=finished_at,
213	                exit_code=result.exit_code,
214	                final_linear_state=post_spawn_state,
215	                worktree_path=str(worktree_path),
216	                halt_reason=None,
217	                **_worker_log_fields(result),
218	            )
219	            worktree.remove(target_repo, worktree_path)
220	            print(f"drain-cycle: {identifier} done; worktree removed.", file=sys.stderr)
221	
222	            # Cycle-wide circuit breaker: every issue may stay under its own
223	            # per-issue caps while their sum drains the quota. Check the
224	            # running totals (which now include this Done issue) before
225	            # spawning the next one; on breach, stop the cycle.
226	            cycle_breach = check_cycle(
227	                limits,
228	                tokens=log.cycle_tokens_cumulative(),
229	                cost_usd=log.cycle_cost_usd(),
230	                seconds=log.cycle_duration_seconds(),
231	            )
232	            if cycle_breach is not None:
233	                halt_reason = f"Halt: {cycle_breach.describe()}"
234	                log.set_cycle_halt(halt_reason)
235	                print(halt_reason, file=sys.stderr)
236	                return 1
237	            continue
238	
239	        # Not-Done halt: revert to the pre-halt state so a re-run picks
240	        # this issue back up instead of silently skipping it.
241	        original_state_name = issue["state"]["name"]
242	        effective_state, revert_error = _revert_to_pre_halt_state(
243	            issue["id"],
244	            target_state_name=original_state_name,
245	            pre_revert_state_name=post_spawn_state,
246	        )
247	        halt_reason = _halt_message(identifier, effective_state, worktree_path)
248	        if revert_error is not None:
249	            halt_reason += (
250	                f" — revert to {original_state_name!r} failed: {revert_error}"
251	            )
252	        # halt_reason carries the same string also printed to stderr below
253	        # so the on-disk and terminal surfaces cannot drift.
254	        log.append_entry(
255	            issue_identifier=identifier,
256	            started_at=started_at,
257	            finished_at=finished_at,
258	            exit_code=result.exit_code,
259	            final_linear_state=effective_state,
260	            worktree_path=str(worktree_path),
261	            halt_reason=halt_reason,
262	            **_worker_log_fields(result),
263	        )
264	        print(halt_reason, file=sys.stderr)
265	        return 1
266	
267	    return 0
268	
```

1	"""Per-cycle run-log artefact.
2	
3	Each ``drain-cycle`` invocation produces a single JSON file at
4	``~/.drain-cycle/runs/<cycle-id>-<run-timestamp>.json`` capturing one
5	entry per attempted issue. The filename embeds the run-start timestamp
6	(UTC, ``%Y%m%dT%H%M%S%fZ``) so re-running ``drain-cycle`` against the
7	same cycle writes a new file instead of clobbering the prior one.
8	Downstream consumers glob the directory and group by ``cycle_id``
9	(carried inside each file).
10	
11	* KR1 (completion %) is computed from ``entries[].final_linear_state``
12	  merged across every file sharing the same ``cycle_id``.
13	* Cycle-drain self-grading reads every file across cycles.
14	* The kill condition (KR1 < 50%) merges per-cycle then thresholds.
15	
16	Schema:
17	
18	::
19	
20	    {
21	      "cycle_id":               "<linear-cycle-uuid>",
22	      "cycle_duration_seconds": <float>,
23	      "cycle_cost_usd":         <float>,
24	      "cycle_tokens_cumulative": <int>,
25	      "cycle_halt_reason":      null | "<cycle-budget halt line>",
26	      "entries":                [
27	        {
28	          "issue_identifier":   "ABA-NNN",
29	          "started_at":         "<iso-8601 UTC>",
30	          "finished_at":        "<iso-8601 UTC>",
31	          "exit_code":          <int>,
32	          "final_linear_state": "Done" | "Todo" | ...,
33	          "worktree_path":      "<absolute path>",
34	          "halt_reason":        null | "<orchestrator stderr halt line>",
35	          "duration_seconds":   <float>,
36	          "model":              null | "<resolved model id>",
37	          "usage":              null | {
38	            "input_tokens":               <int>,
39	            "output_tokens":              <int>,
40	            "cache_creation_input_tokens": <int>,
41	            "cache_read_input_tokens":    <int>,
42	            "cumulative":                 <int>,
43	            "peak_context":               <int>,
44	          },
45	          "cost_usd":           null | <float>,
46	          "num_turns":          null | <int>,
47	          "session_id":         null | "<uuid>",
48	          "is_error":           null | <bool>,
49	        },
50	        ...
51	      ],
52	    }
53	
54	``halt_reason`` is ``null`` on every Done entry (the agent flipped state
55	as required). On the orchestrator's halt entry it is the exact string
56	also written to stderr — both surfaces are produced by the same
57	``_halt_message`` helper in ``orchestrator.py`` so the on-disk and
58	terminal values cannot drift. Non-last entries are
59	``null`` by construction: the orchestrator returns on first halt, so
60	anything before the halt is a Done entry.
61	
62	The worker-usage fields (``model`` through ``is_error``) are populated
63	from the spawned session's stream-json events — see ``worker.py``.
64	Entries written before any session runs (pre-spawn resolution and
65	setup-failure halts) carry ``null`` for ``model`` / ``usage`` /
66	``cost_usd`` / ``num_turns`` / ``session_id`` / ``is_error``; every entry
67	keeps the same key set. ``usage.cumulative`` is the billed-token total
68	across turns; ``usage.peak_context`` is the largest single-turn context.
69	These fields are additive — ``grade.py`` reads only ``cycle_id`` and
70	``entries[].final_linear_state`` / ``exit_code``, so existing run logs
71	without them grade unchanged.
72	
73	``cycle_cost_usd`` and ``cycle_tokens_cumulative`` are per-invocation
74	totals over ``entries`` (entries with ``null`` cost or usage contribute
75	zero), recomputed on every persist alongside ``cycle_duration_seconds``.
76	
77	``cycle_halt_reason`` is ``null`` unless the orchestrator stopped the run
78	because a *cycle-wide* cap (tokens / cost / wall-clock) was breached — the
79	death-by-aggregate case where every issue stayed under its own per-issue
80	caps but their sum crossed the cycle budget. The breaching issue's own
81	entry is recorded normally (it ran to Done); this top-level field is the
82	record of why no further issue was attempted, carrying the same ``Halt:``
83	string printed to stderr. A per-issue breach, by contrast, lands in the
84	halting entry's ``halt_reason`` like any other halt.
85	
86	``cycle_duration_seconds`` is computed automatically on every persist as
87	``max(finished_at) - min(started_at)`` across ``entries`` (``0.0`` when
88	empty). This is the KR2 grading proxy — "how long was the agent doing
89	execution for me unattended" stands in for "operator hands-off time" —
90	and replaces the originally specified ``time_spent`` block, which
91	required manual self-report at cycle close and was never going to be
92	filled in.
93	
94	Persistence is incremental: ``append_entry`` re-serialises the whole dict
95	on every call, so a mid-run crash still leaves a well-formed file with
96	every issue attempted so far. Cycle scale (≤ 15 issues) makes the cost
97	irrelevant.
98	"""
99	from __future__ import annotations
100	
101	import json
102	from dataclasses import dataclass, field
103	from datetime import datetime, timezone
104	from pathlib import Path
105	from typing import Any
106	
107	
108	def runs_dir() -> Path:
109	    """Return the directory where per-cycle run logs are written.
110	
111	    Resolved on every call (not module-import time) so tests can redirect
112	    via ``monkeypatch.setenv("HOME", ...)`` after the module is imported.
113	    """
114	    return Path.home() / ".drain-cycle" / "runs"
115	
116	
117	@dataclass
118	class RunLog:
119	    """Per-cycle run-log file. Construct once at orchestrator start.
120	
121	    Writing the initial ``{cycle_id, cycle_duration_seconds: 0.0,
122	    entries: []}`` shell in ``__post_init__`` means that even a zero-issue
123	    cycle (or a crash before the first ``append_entry``) leaves the
124	    artefact on disk, which is what kill-condition tooling needs to
125	    distinguish "drained nothing" from "never ran".
126	
127	    The filename embeds a UTC run-start timestamp with microsecond
128	    resolution, so two ``RunLog`` instances on the same ``cycle_id``
129	    (re-running ``drain-cycle`` after a halt) write to two separate
130	    files instead of one clobbering the other.
131	    """
132	
133	    cycle_id: str
134	    path: Path = field(init=False)
135	    entries: list[dict[str, Any]] = field(default_factory=list, init=False)
136	    cycle_halt_reason: str | None = field(default=None, init=False)
137	
138	    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
139	        directory = runs_dir()
140	        directory.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
141	        run_started_at = datetime.now(tz=timezone.utc)
142	        timestamp = run_started_at.strftime("%Y%m%dT%H%M%S%fZ")
143	        self.path = directory / f"{self.cycle_id}-{timestamp}.json"
144	        self._persist()
145	
146	    def append_entry(
147	        self,
148	        *,
149	        issue_identifier: str,
150	        started_at: str,
151	        finished_at: str,
152	        exit_code: int,
153	        final_linear_state: str,
154	        worktree_path: str,
155	        halt_reason: str | None = None,
156	        duration_seconds: float | None = None,
157	        model: str | None = None,
158	        usage: dict[str, int] | None = None,
159	        cost_usd: float | None = None,
160	        num_turns: int | None = None,
161	        session_id: str | None = None,
162	        is_error: bool | None = None,
163	    ) -> None:
164	        if duration_seconds is None:
165	            duration_seconds = (
166	                datetime.fromisoformat(finished_at)
167	                - datetime.fromisoformat(started_at)
168	            ).total_seconds()
169	        self.entries.append(
170	            {
171	                "issue_identifier": issue_identifier,
172	                "started_at": started_at,
173	                "finished_at": finished_at,
174	                "exit_code": exit_code,
175	                "final_linear_state": final_linear_state,
176	                "worktree_path": worktree_path,
177	                "halt_reason": halt_reason,
178	                "duration_seconds": duration_seconds,
179	                "model": model,
180	                "usage": usage,
181	                "cost_usd": cost_usd,
182	                "num_turns": num_turns,
183	                "session_id": session_id,
184	                "is_error": is_error,
185	            }
186	        )
187	        self._persist()
188	
189	    def set_cycle_halt(self, reason: str) -> None:
190	        """Record why a cycle-wide cap stopped the run, and persist.
191	
192	        Called by the orchestrator after the breaching issue's own entry is
193	        already written, so the file carries both the issue's normal entry
194	        and the cycle-level reason no further issue was attempted.
195	        """
196	        self.cycle_halt_reason = reason
197	        self._persist()
198	
199	    def cycle_duration_seconds(self) -> float:
200	        if not self.entries:
201	            return 0.0
202	        starts = [datetime.fromisoformat(e["started_at"]) for e in self.entries]
203	        finishes = [datetime.fromisoformat(e["finished_at"]) for e in self.entries]
204	        return (max(finishes) - min(starts)).total_seconds()
205	
206	    def cycle_cost_usd(self) -> float:
207	        return sum(
208	            e["cost_usd"] for e in self.entries if e.get("cost_usd") is not None
209	        )
210	
211	    def cycle_tokens_cumulative(self) -> int:
212	        return sum(
213	            e["usage"]["cumulative"]
214	            for e in self.entries
215	            if e.get("usage") is not None
216	        )
217	
218	    def _persist(self) -> None:
219	        payload = {
220	            "cycle_id": self.cycle_id,
221	            "cycle_duration_seconds": self.cycle_duration_seconds(),
222	            "cycle_cost_usd": self.cycle_cost_usd(),
223	            "cycle_tokens_cumulative": self.cycle_tokens_cumulative(),
224	            "cycle_halt_reason": self.cycle_halt_reason,
225	            "entries": self.entries,
226	        }
227	        self.path.write_text(json.dumps(payload, indent=2) + "\n")
228	
```

Usage: claude [options] [command] [prompt]

Claude Code - starts an interactive session by default, use -p/--print for
non-interactive output

Arguments:
  prompt                                            Your prompt

Options:
  --add-dir <directories...>                        Additional directories to allow tool access to
  --agent <agent>                                   Agent for the current session. Overrides the 'agent' setting.
  --agents <json>                                   JSON object defining custom agents (e.g. '{"reviewer": {"description": "Reviews code", "prompt": "You are a code reviewer"}}')
  --allow-dangerously-skip-permissions              Enable bypassing all permission checks as an option, without it being enabled by default. Recommended only for sandboxes with no internet access.
  --allowedTools, --allowed-tools <tools...>        Comma or space-separated list of tool names to allow (e.g. "Bash(git *) Edit")
  --append-system-prompt <prompt>                   Append a system prompt to the default system prompt
  --bare                                            Minimal mode: skip hooks, LSP, plugin sync, attribution, auto-memory, background prefetches, keychain reads, and CLAUDE.md auto-discovery. Sets CLAUDE_CODE_SIMPLE=1. Anthropic auth is strictly ANTHROPIC_API_KEY or apiKeyHelper via --settings (OAuth and keychain are never read). 3P providers (Bedrock/Vertex/Foundry) use their own credentials. Skills still resolve via /skill-name. Explicitly provide context via: --system-prompt[-file], --append-system-prompt[-file], --add-dir (CLAUDE.md dirs), --mcp-config, --settings, --agents, --plugin-dir.
  --betas <betas...>                                Beta headers to include in API requests (API key users only)
  --brief                                           Enable SendUserMessage tool for agent-to-user communication
  --chrome                                          Enable Claude in Chrome integration
  -c, --continue                                    Continue the most recent conversation in the current directory
  --dangerously-skip-permissions                    Bypass all permission checks. Recommended only for sandboxes with no internet access.
  -d, --debug [filter]                              Enable debug mode with optional category filtering (e.g., "api,hooks" or "!1p,!file")
  --debug-file <path>                               Write debug logs to a specific file path (implicitly enables debug mode)
  --disable-slash-commands                          Disable all skills
  --disallowedTools, --disallowed-tools <tools...>  Comma or space-separated list of tool names to deny (e.g. "Bash(git *) Edit")
  --effort <level>                                  Effort level for the current session (low, medium, high, xhigh, max)
  --exclude-dynamic-system-prompt-sections          Move per-machine sections (cwd, env info, memory paths, git status) from the system prompt into the first user message. Improves cross-user prompt-cache reuse. Only applies with the default system prompt (ignored with --system-prompt). (default: false)
  --fallback-model <model>                          Enable automatic fallback to specified model when default model is overloaded (only works with --print)
  --file <specs...>                                 File resources to download at startup. Format: file_id:relative_path (e.g., --file file_abc:doc.txt file_def:img.png)
  --fork-session                                    When resuming, create a new session ID instead of reusing the original (use with --resume or --continue)
  --from-pr [value]                                 Resume a session linked to a PR by PR number/URL, or open interactive picker with optional search term
  -h, --help                                        Display help for command
  --ide                                             Automatically connect to IDE on startup if exactly one valid IDE is available
  --include-hook-events                             Include all hook lifecycle events in the output stream (only works with --output-format=stream-json)
  --include-partial-messages                        Include partial message chunks as they arrive (only works with --print and --output-format=stream-json)
  --input-format <format>                           Input format (only works with --print): "text" (default), or "stream-json" (realtime streaming input) (choices: "text", "stream-json")
  --json-schema <schema>                            JSON Schema for structured output validation. Example: {"type":"object","properties":{"name":{"type":"string"}},"required":["name"]}
  --max-budget-usd <amount>                         Maximum dollar amount to spend on API calls (only works with --print)
  --mcp-config <configs...>                         Load MCP servers from JSON files or strings (space-separated)
  --mcp-debug                                       [DEPRECATED. Use --debug instead] Enable MCP debug mode (shows MCP server errors)
  --model <model>                                   Model for the current session. Provide an alias for the latest model (e.g. 'sonnet' or 'opus') or a model's full name (e.g. 'claude-sonnet-4-6').
  -n, --name <name>                                 Set a display name for this session (shown in the prompt box, /resume picker, and terminal title)
  --no-chrome                                       Disable Claude in Chrome integration
  --no-session-persistence                          Disable session persistence - sessions will not be saved to disk and cannot be resumed (only works with --print)
  --output-format <format>                          Output format (only works with --print): "text" (default), "json" (single result), or "stream-json" (realtime streaming) (choices: "text", "json", "stream-json")
  --permission-mode <mode>                          Permission mode to use for the session (choices: "acceptEdits", "auto", "bypassPermissions", "default", "dontAsk", "plan")
  --plugin-dir <path>                               Load a plugin from a directory or .zip for this session only (repeatable: --plugin-dir A --plugin-dir B.zip) (default: [])
  --plugin-url <url>                                Fetch a plugin .zip from a URL for this session only (repeatable: --plugin-url A --plugin-url B) (default: [])
  -p, --print                                       Print response and exit (useful for pipes). Note: The workspace trust dialog is skipped when Claude is run in non-interactive mode (via -p, or when stdout is not a TTY, e.g. piped or redirected output). Only use this in directories you trust. Settings files that fail validation are silently ignored in this mode (no error dialog is shown).
  --remote-control [name]                           Start an interactive session with Remote Control enabled (optionally named)
  --remote-control-session-name-prefix <prefix>     Prefix for auto-generated Remote Control session names (default: hostname)
  --replay-user-messages                            Re-emit user messages from stdin back on stdout for acknowledgment (only works with --input-format=stream-json and --output-format=stream-json)
  -r, --resume [value]                              Resume a conversation by session ID, or open interactive picker with optional search term
  --session-id <uuid>                               Use a specific session ID for the conversation (must be a valid UUID)
  --setting-sources <sources>                       Comma-separated list of setting sources to load (user, project, local).
  --settings <file-or-json>                         Path to a settings JSON file or a JSON string to load additional settings from
  --strict-mcp-config                               Only use MCP servers from --mcp-config, ignoring all other MCP configurations
